# Acorn Web IDE — Delivery TODO and Traceability Backlog

Status: Draft for stakeholder review  
Date: 20 August 2026  
Companion specification: `docs/requirements-specification.md`

## 1. Rules for using this backlog

- [ ] GOV-001 Obtain written acceptance of the requirements specification,
  initial release scope, support-tier language, and this backlog **before any
  product implementation code is written**.
- [ ] GOV-002 Give every delivery issue a requirement ID, acceptance evidence,
  owner, estimate, dependency, risk, and target release.
- [x] GOV-003 Treat checkboxes as incomplete until evidence is linked; a merged
  implementation alone is not completion.
  - [x] Evidence: The traceability report generated by DOC-904 counts this
   directly and lists by name every completed requirement that records no
   verification. It found 35, each of which has since been given an Evidence
   entry whose counts were taken from running the suites rather than
   estimated. Untraced has stayed at zero as the backlog has grown: the report
   currently shows 77 traced, 57 described and 0 untraced across 134 completed
   requirements of 294 tracked. Those numbers are regenerated rather than
   maintained — `npm run traceability` rewrites the report, and its contract
   runs in the release gate — so a tick added without evidence appears in the
   next report rather than passing unnoticed, and the figures quoted here are
   a snapshot of a document that is the authority.
- [ ] GOV-004 Maintain one requirement-to-design-to-test traceability matrix.
- [ ] GOV-005 Record scope changes in the specification and an accepted decision,
  not only in an issue or chat.
- [ ] GOV-006 Preserve honest `experimental`, `preview`, and `production` labels;
  never satisfy target support with a non-functional selector.
- [ ] GOV-007 Review architecture, accessibility, security, privacy, legal,
  operations, and test impact at each release gate.

## 2. Phase 0 — product, legal, and feasibility discovery

### 2.1 Product definition

- [ ] P0-001 Name the product and approve an original Acorn-inspired identity
  that does not copy Web64 or third-party trademarks/artwork (DEC-001, UX-004).
- [ ] P0-002 Validate personas and journeys through interviews/tasks covering a
  beginner, 6502 expert, RISC OS developer, educator, porter, and assistive-
  technology users (P-01–P-07, J-01–J-08).
- [ ] P0-003 Record the exact public-release target profiles and the definition of
  “supports the Acorn line” (DEC-002, ACC-001–ACC-003).
- [ ] P0-004 Convert the Web64 baseline table into demonstration scripts and
  acceptance scenarios; note any approved deferment (Section 6).
- [ ] P0-005 Define analytics/feedback needs with privacy minimization; analytics
  cannot be a production prerequisite for local mode (SEC-006, CLD-001).

    compatibility workflow. The implemented BASIC and cc65 C decisions are
    recorded below; ARM and RISC OS inventories remain open, so the parent
    inventory is not complete. The bounded GNU ARM2 assembler/linker decision
    is now recorded in ADR 0005; RISC OS C and application packaging remain
    open.
- [ ] P0-014 Prove diagnostic parsing, label/source-map generation, deterministic
  output, cancellation, sandboxing, licence, and version pinning for each

  - [x] The ca65/ld65 vertical slice pins Debian cc65 `2.19-1`, records both

  - [x] The separate BeebAsm 1.11 slice pins upstream commit

  - [x] The cc65 C slice audits Debian `2.19-1` and current upstream commit

### Phase 0 exit gate

- [ ] P0-GATE Requirements and backlog accepted; selected initial target slices
  have credible emulator/toolchain/media/debug paths; licences and ROM handling
  are understood; threat model and architecture ADR set are approved. Only then
  may implementation begin.

## 3. Phase 1 — experience and design system foundation

### 3.1 Information architecture and prototypes

- [ ] UX-100 Map project, workspace, asset, emulator, debugger, inspector,
  research, settings, account, and status information architecture (UX-001).
- [ ] UX-101 Prototype desktop workbench at reference and minimum supported
  resolutions with resizable/collapsible/movable panels (UX-001–UX-003).
- [ ] UX-102 Prototype narrow reflow at 320 CSS px, 200% text zoom, mobile/tablet,
  and browser zoom without two-dimensional page scrolling (UX-003, UX-006).
- [ ] UX-103 Define global action bar order and state for new/open/save/import/
  export/build/run/cloud/about/help and accessible overflow behavior.
- [ ] UX-104 Define target/configuration selector, comparison view, compatibility
  warnings, support badges, ROM prerequisites, and profile manifest summary.
- [ ] UX-105 Define focus movement across docks, tabs, toolbars, trees, editor,
  emulator, canvas alternatives, inspector, modals, and notifications.
- [ ] UX-106 Define command palette taxonomy and default/remappable shortcuts,
  including conflict detection with browser and emulated keyboards (EDT-008).
  - [x] `src/commands/keyBindings.ts` is now the single declared inventory of
    dispatched shortcuts, covering the twenty workbench chords and the
    forty-four source-editor chords. It normalizes events to a canonical
    `Ctrl+Alt+Shift+Key` chord from the physical key code, so shifted
    punctuation and non-US layouts resolve to the configured chord and Command
    on macOS shares the Control role. The workbench window handler, the editor
    handler, the palette's advertised chords and the editor's
    `aria-keyshortcuts` list are all generated from the resolved table, which
    removed two separately maintained hard-coded chord chains. Two documented
    defects were fixed by exact-chord dispatch: `Ctrl/Cmd+Shift+F` was consumed
    by the editor's local find instead of reaching project search, and the help
    guide advertised a `Ctrl+K` palette chord that nothing dispatched.
  - [x] Settings › Keyboard shortcuts lists both scopes with effective and
    default chords, records a replacement chord, unbinds a command and resets
    one or all bindings. Assignment is refused only for chords that would
    capture ordinary typing or Tab focus movement. Same-scope conflicts, editor
    chords that hide a workbench chord, and chords a browser normally claims
    first are named rather than silently prevented or falsely promised as
    captured. Overrides are validated against the declared inventory and
    persisted in a versioned browser-storage envelope.
  - [x] Evidence: 19 model contracts in `src/commands/keyBindings.test.ts`,
    7 panel contracts in `src/components/KeyboardShortcutsPanel.test.tsx`, and
    6 real-dispatch contracts in `src/components/keyBindingDispatch.test.tsx`
    proving a remapped chord runs the command, the replaced default stops
    running it, an unbound command stops dispatching and an unclaimed chord is
    left to the browser. Frontend suite 121 files / 600 tests, TypeScript and
    production Vite builds pass. A headed Chromium run against the production
    `dist` bundle from a clean browser profile opened Settings, listed all 64
    declared bindings as defaults, recorded `Ctrl+Alt+7` from a genuine browser
    key event, applied it, persisted
    `{"version":1,"overrides":{"workbench.build-active":"Ctrl+Alt+7"}}`,
    retained the chord across a full reload, showed the command palette
    advertising the replacement rather than `F7`, then remapped the palette
    itself and proved that the replaced `F1` default no longer opened it while
    the new `Ctrl+Alt+5` chord did. Zero console or page errors were recorded.
  - [ ] Emulated-keyboard conflict detection, chorded key sequences, separate
    Command and Control assignment, and the palette taxonomy review with
    accessibility reviewers keep this item open.
- [ ] UX-107 User-test J-01, J-04, J-05, and J-07 prototypes with relevant
  personas and revise before component implementation.

### 3.3 Accessibility test foundation

- [x] A11Y-100 Publish WCAG 2.2 AA conformance checklist mapped to components and
  journeys (UX-006–UX-011, QLT-005).
  - [x] `docs/accessibility-conformance.md` lists all 55 Level A and AA success
    criteria of WCAG 2.2, each with how it is verified rather than with a
    claim: 13 decided automatically by a named release-gate rule, 9 partly, 22
    by a person with the step stated, and 11 not applicable with the absence
    that makes them so. A conformance claim is worth exactly as much as the
    evidence behind it, so the document records the evidence.
  - [x] The distinction carrying the most weight is between automated and
    manual, and it is enforced rather than described. A scan can find a control
    with no accessible name; it cannot tell whether the name means anything,
    and recording the second as though the first covered it is how a product
    ends up believing it is accessible. A contract requires every automated
    entry to name the rule behind it, every partial entry to say which half is
    manual, and every not-applicable entry to name what the product does not
    have and explain it — that last being the entry easiest to abuse.
  - [x] Writing it caught two citation errors in the accessibility rules
    themselves. Duplicate identifiers were cited against 4.1.1 Parsing, which
    WCAG 2.2 removed; they are now cited against 4.1.2, because what still
    matters is that an aria-labelledby reference to a duplicated id is
    ambiguous. Reduced motion was cited against 2.3.3, which is Level AAA; the
    gate now says so plainly and treats honouring the preference as a product
    commitment rather than an AA obligation, with 2.2.2 Pause, Stop, Hide
    recorded as the criterion that does apply.
  - [x] The document makes no overall conformance claim while the manual matrix
    is unrun, and says that too. Level AAA is out of scope, and where the
    product happens to meet a AAA criterion it is not counted, because meeting
    one criterion of a level is not conformance to it.
  - [x] Evidence: 12 contracts covering the checked-in document matching the
    generator, internal consistency, all 55 criteria present exactly once, 4.1.1
    absent because WCAG 2.2 removed it, all six criteria WCAG 2.2 added being
    present, both validator rules that keep an entry honest, every automated
    claim naming a rule, every limitation the scan declares appearing as manual
    work somewhere, the counts adding up, and the absence of an overall claim.
    `npm run conformance` regenerates it.
- [x] A11Y-101 Add static linting and automated browser accessibility scans with
  explicit limitations and blocker thresholds.
  - [x] The release gate now scans every workspace the tab strip offers —
    eighteen of them, read from the page so a new one is covered the day it is
    added — against eight rules, each naming the WCAG success criterion it
    checks so a finding traces to the requirement rather than to somebody's
    preference: controls and images without an accessible name, duplicate
    identifiers, document language and title, heading levels that skip,
    landmarks a screen reader could not tell apart, text contrast against its
    own background, targets below the minimum size, and any claim of the
    essential exception made without a reason.
  - [x] The limitation is stated rather than implied. The scan declares what it
    cannot decide — whether a name is meaningful rather than merely present,
    whether a reading order makes sense, whether a live region announces at a
    useful moment, whether an alternative conveys what the visual did, and
    screen-reader behaviour itself — and those stay in the manual matrix. An
    automated scan that implied full coverage would be worse than none.
  - [x] The threshold is zero, and it is real: the first run found 126
    findings across eleven distinct problems, and the gate stayed red until
    every one was fixed or exempted with a reason. Verified in both directions
    — darkening one colour token produced 324 findings and restoring it
    returned to zero, so the scan can fail and a passing run means something.
  - [x] What it found and what was done about it. One colour token was at
    3.96:1 in the dark theme and 3.08:1 in the light one, failing 1.4.3 in
    every workspace; both were recomputed to clear 4.5:1 on the lightest
    surface each appears on. Palette swatches wrote their index on a colour the
    user chose, measuring 1.03:1 — text the same colour as what it sits on —
    and now pick black or white per swatch by luminance, which is the only
    approach that works for a colour the product does not choose. Fifty-seven
    controls were 22 pixels tall against the 24 that 2.5.8 requires, a failure
    for the sake of two pixels, so the minimum is now set once for the shell
    rather than corrected control by control. Five workspaces jumped from the
    page heading straight to level four. The selected map brush wrote cream on
    bright green at 1.57:1. The help navigation used the faint token on a
    lighter surface than it was tuned for.
  - [x] The pixel and glyph grids claim 2.5.8's own essential exception, and
    claim it in the markup with the reason attached rather than by a class name
    the scanner guesses at, so the exemption is reviewable where it is made —
    and an exemption claimed without a reason is itself reported. A cell in
    those grids is one pixel of the artwork, and enlarging it past the artwork
    would change what the editor edits.
  - [x] Evidence: 11 rule contracts driving the same expression the gate runs,
    covering every accepted way of giving a name, both exemption paths, the
    boundary at exactly the minimum size, something that cannot be hit at its
    own centre, and the grouped report; plus the live scan in the release gate
    across eighteen workspaces.
- [x] A11Y-102 Build keyboard-only scripts for each workbench surface and all
  destructive/drag alternatives.
  - [x] Every workspace is now checked for keyboard operation in the release
    gate, not one screen but all eighteen. Two things are checked together
    because they are halves of the same question: a control no keyboard route
    reaches cannot be operated at all, and focus that enters something and
    cannot leave takes the rest of the product with it.
  - [x] Tab order is deliberately not treated as the whole answer. A tree, a
    grid and a tab strip hold one tab stop and move within themselves using the
    arrow keys, which is the pattern those roles prescribe; counting their rows
    as missing tab stops would report correct code as broken. What is checked
    is that every group holding focusable content is itself reachable, and that
    focus can always leave wherever it is — with the trap check skipped when
    there is only one stop, since then there is nowhere to go and the check
    would only be reporting that the page is small.
  - [x] Dragging cannot be done without a pointer, so anything draggable has to
    state its alternative, declared in the markup next to the thing that needs
    it rather than listed somewhere a reviewer has to go and find. The project
    tree declares Alt with an arrow, which is the same operation a drag
    performs. An alternative claimed without being stated fails, because a
    claim nobody can check is not an alternative. Every destructive action —
    delete, remove, purge, discard, erase, clear, empty, revert — is checked to
    be reachable from the keyboard.
  - [x] The rule is refused the chance to pass vacuously: the gate counts what
    was draggable while the scan ran and fails if the answer is none, and it
    opens the project explorer first, because a panel that happens to be closed
    takes everything inside it out of the scan without saying so. Verified by
    removing the tree's declaration, which produced the finding, and restoring
    it, which cleared it.
  - [x] The gate now opens a sample project through the real dialog before it
    scans, because most of the workbench has nothing to show until something is
    loaded and scanning the empty state reported a clean page while leaving the
    surfaces that carry the most information unmeasured. Coverage went from 95
    controls to 319, and the first run of the larger scan immediately found a
    real failure the empty state had hidden: a button in an empty state had no
    styling at all, fell back to the browser's own grey, and put faint text on
    it at 1.77:1. It is a real control and is now dressed as one.
  - [x] Evidence: 7 further contracts covering an unreachable group, a roving
    tab stop being accepted, a page with no tab stop at all, dragging with no
    alternative, an alternative claimed without being stated, a stated one, and
    a destructive action the keyboard cannot reach.
- [ ] A11Y-103 Establish manual screen-reader matrix and recruit representative
  testers before feature freeze.
- [x] A11Y-104 Add zoom, text spacing, reflow, forced-colours, reduced-motion,
  reduced-transparency, focus visibility, and touch-target checks.
  - [x] Zoom, reflow and touch targets are in the release gate. The workbench
    is measured at five sizes down to the 320 CSS pixels WCAG 1.4.10 names —
    which is also 400% zoom on a 1280-pixel display — with no horizontal
    scrolling, nothing out of reach and no control clipped; and every pointer
    target is measured against the 24-pixel minimum, with the essential
    exception declared in the markup where it is claimed.
  - [x] Text spacing, forced colours, reduced motion and focus visibility are
    now checked with the condition actually turned on rather than by reading
    the stylesheet, because a rule that declares support for one of these looks
    correct in the source and can still do nothing on screen. Text spacing
    applies the overrides WCAG 1.4.12 names — line height one and a half times,
    paragraphs twice, letters and words spaced — and measures every element
    that holds text for content taller than a box that cannot scroll. Focus
    visibility focuses each control in turn and compares its outline, shadow,
    border, background and underline against the same element unfocused:
    something has to change, because a change nobody can see is the same as no
    change. Reduced motion is emulated and anything still animating for longer
    than a twentieth of a second is reported. Forced colours is emulated and
    every control is checked to have a boundary that survives the system
    replacing the palette.
  - [x] Forced colours found a real gap: the activity rail, the plain icon
    buttons, the collapse and emulator controls and the trash actions had no
    border, outline, underline or text, so once the system supplied the colours
    nothing marked them as controls at all. They now take a border in that mode
    only, the active rail button and workspace tab take a Highlight outline,
    and the range, colour and file inputs — which draw their own track and
    button that the system replaces — take one too.
  - [x] Evidence: 6 further contracts covering text a fixed box cuts off, a
    container whose children scroll being left alone, something clipped to a
    pixel for a screen reader being left alone, a control that looks identical
    focused and unfocused, motion that survives the preference and motion too
    brief to count, and an icon-only control with nothing marking it as one;
    plus all four conditions emulated in the release gate.
  - [x] Reduced transparency is now checked rather than asserted. The position
    recorded here was that the product carries no meaning in translucency, and
    a position is not a check: the same sentence would have been written on the
    day it stopped being true. The condition is now emulated in the release gate
    with a rule that reports anything read *through* translucency — a
    translucent background, or a backdrop filter — and any element opacity that
    is not a disabled or unavailable control.
  - [x] The scope of that rule was measured before it was written rather than
    guessed. Against the built workbench, thirty-six elements are drawn at
    partial opacity and every one of them is a disabled or planned control;
    there is no translucent background and no backdrop filter anywhere. Dimming
    a control that cannot be used is a convention with a meaning of its own and
    is not what this preference is about, so reporting all thirty-six would have
    buried the thing the rule exists for under entries that are all correct. The
    recorded position turns out to be right about the half that matters, and the
    check is what keeps it right.
  - [x] Proved by deliberate breakage against the running product, not only in
    jsdom: the rule reports nothing on the workbench as it stands, and reports
    the panel the moment a translucent one with a backdrop filter is put into
    the live page. Evidence: 6 further contracts covering partial opacity, a
    background with an alpha channel, a backdrop filter, a dimmed disabled
    control being left alone, a translucent background on a disabled control
    still being reported, something declared decorative being left alone with
    everything inside it, and something fully transparent not being mistaken for
    translucency.

### Phase 1 exit gate

- [ ] UX-GATE Stakeholders and accessibility reviewers accept an interactive
  prototype for desktop and reflow; shared component contracts cover every
  interaction required by the first vertical slice.

## 4. Phase 2 — platform skeleton and local project loop

### 4.1 Repository and deployable foundations

- [ ] PLAT-200 Scaffold React/TypeScript/Vite client and supported PHP/Symfony
  services only after P0-GATE (ARC-02–ARC-04).
- [x] PLAT-201 Provide Docker Compose, container health checks, migrations,
  named development volumes, safe `.env.example`, and fresh-clone startup.
  - [x] Two services in `compose.yaml`, plus a third behind a CI profile. The
    native builder has no network route at all, drops every capability, runs
    read-only as an unprivileged user, and is bounded on memory, processes, CPU
    and temporary space. The workbench depends on it being healthy rather than
    merely started.
  - [x] Both services declare health checks and both were observed reaching
    healthy from a clean start. `GET /healthz` returns 200 with `ok`. The socket
    between them is a named volume, and the CI runner is attached only to an
    internal network with no external route.
  - [x] There are no migrations, and that is a property rather than an omission:
    the product has no database. A project lives in the browser, and the builder
    keeps nothing between builds. `.env.example` carries one variable, the host
    port, and no value that could be a secret — the hygiene scanner tells a
    template apart from a real `.env` and still reads it for content.
  - [x] Evidence: the full lifecycle run recorded under DOC-903, which built
    both images from a clean state, started them, confirmed health, the served
    security policies, readable logs and the absence of any firmware in the
    image, ran a headless browser against the running container at five sizes,
    and shut down leaving nothing behind. That run also found and fixed two real
    defects in the documented path.
- [ ] PLAT-202 Establish formatting, static analysis, type checks, unit/
  integration/E2E test commands, coverage policy, and CI quality gates.
  - [x] One gate, `npm run ci`, is the single definition of what must pass, so a
    developer's machine and the pipeline check the same things. Seven stages:
    the TypeScript project build, help verification, the whole test suite, the
    production build with its four runtime documents present, the vendored GPL
    provenance, an executable SEC-903 check that no firmware or media image is
    tracked, and a headless browser smoke that boots the built workbench and
    fails on any console error.
  - [x] A stage that cannot run is reported skipped with the reason and fails
    the gate, so a pipeline cannot drift into checking less than it believes.
    `--allow-skips` is the explicit way to accept that. A stage filter matching
    nothing fails rather than reading as a pass, and a named `CHROMIUM_PATH`
    that is not there is a skip rather than a silent fallback to another
    browser. All four exit paths were exercised.
  - [x] A GitHub Actions workflow installs a browser, runs that one gate and
    publishes its JSON report as an artifact. It was written for a monorepo and
    lived at the parent repository's root, path-filtered to this service; when
    this project became a repository of its own that workflow did not come with
    it, and the published repository had no CI at all until the filters and the
    working-directory prefixes were removed and it was brought here as
    `.github/workflows/release-gate.yml`. The steps are unchanged, because
    `npm run ci` remains the one definition of what has to pass.
  - [x] Two defects in the gate itself were found by running it. It computed its
    own root from `import.meta.url.pathname`, which percent-encodes the space in
    this repository's path, so every spawned command failed with ENOENT; and it
    printed its verdict without closing the smoke stage's WebSocket, so the
    process never exited and would have hung a pipeline until its timeout.
  - [x] A third, found the same way, and the worst of them: the gate could
    measure a browser it had not started. Chromium hands its command line to an
    existing instance holding the same profile and then exits, and the debugging
    port answers either way, so asking only whether the port responds is not
    asking whose browser it is. A browser left behind by an earlier run was
    found still holding that port a day later. The gate attached to it, reported
    a first-render control count three times the true figure because the page it
    measured had a project open from the previous day, and eventually hung
    outright — the failure this stage exists to prevent, produced by the stage
    itself.
  - [x] The port is now refused before anything is launched if something is
    already answering on it, naming the port and how to clear it; a browser that
    exits before accepting a connection — which is exactly what Chromium does on
    that hand-off — is reported rather than worked around; and any profile left
    by an earlier run is removed before launch, with the run refused if it
    cannot be, so starting from a browser that has seen nothing is a guarantee
    rather than a hope. Teardown waits for the browser to exit, escalating to
    SIGKILL, instead of signalling it and deleting the directory underneath it.
  - [x] Evidence: both refusals were proved by deliberate breakage rather than
    by reading the code — port 9137 was occupied by a foreign browser, and a
    profile directory was made unremovable. Each produced its own message and
    neither hung. The first-render count is now stable at 95 across consecutive
    runs, where it had been drifting upward as state accumulated; the 319 that
    earlier runs reported was never the workbench's first render.
  - [x] No test is allowed to skip. Three backend test classes previously
    excused themselves when a toolchain was absent, and two of them pointed at
    hard-coded paths that existed on exactly one machine. A test that only runs
    somewhere is worse than no test: the suite reports green while checking less
    than it claims. Every `markTestSkipped` is gone; a missing toolchain now
    fails with the two ways to obtain it.
  - [x] `npm run toolchains` makes them present rather than hoped for. It reads
    the pinned commit and version out of the product's own manifest so it cannot
    drift, clones BeebAsm, verifies the checked-out commit is that commit,
    builds it with the same target and flags the container image uses, and
    refuses a binary that does not report the pinned version. That mattered
    immediately: this machine had a system BeebAsm 1.10, which assembles the
    same source into different bytes than the pinned 1.11 the tests assert. It
    also locates cc65 and the GNU ARM binutils wherever they are, rather than
    insisting on one directory, and reports the version it found.
  - [x] `npm run test:backend` publishes those paths, runs the suite and then
    fails if anything was skipped. The gate runs it as its own stage, and the
    JavaScript stage refuses a skipped test the same way.
  - [x] Two skips in disguise were removed from the JavaScript suite as well:
    conditional early returns that would have silently dropped the rest of a
    test body. Both are now assertions, so the condition failing fails the test.
  - [x] The result: 55 backend tests and 149 assertions, none skipped, against
    BeebAsm 1.11 at the pinned commit, cc65 and GNU ARM binutils. Assertions
    rose from 134 to 149 because the BeebAsm tests are now actually executing.
  - [x] Evidence: a full local run reported `8 passed · 0 failed · 0 skipped`,
    covering 1,043 browser tests and 55 backend tests with nothing skipped in
    either, and a workbench that rendered 95 controls with no console errors.
  - [x] A coverage policy is now part of the gate, measured in the same run as
    the suite rather than a second pass. The floors — 65% statements and lines,
    80% branches and functions — sit just under what the suite achieves today,
    which is deliberate: coverage is a floor against regression and not a target
    to chase. A high figure says a line ran, never that anything was checked
    about it, so a floor set at what is already true catches code added without
    tests and gives nobody a reason to write tests at a number. The gate fails
    if the suite runs and writes no coverage summary at all, because a
    measurement that did not happen must not read as one that passed.
  - [x] What is measured is what the product does. Generated documents are
    data rather than behaviour and are excluded, as is the application entry
    point and the sample projects, so the figure is not inflated by files whose
    correctness is settled elsewhere. Current: 67.33% statements, 82.69%
    branches, 81.99% functions across 1,507 tests.
  - [ ] Formatting and lint rules, and static analysis for the PHP backend, are
    not yet part of the gate. TypeScript runs in strict mode with unused locals
    and parameters treated as errors, which covers part of what a linter would,
    but that is not the same thing and is not claimed as it.
- [ ] PLAT-203 Generate typed client contracts from the accepted API description
  and add server/client compatibility tests (API-002).
- [x] PLAT-204 Implement structured logging/correlation/redaction and baseline
  metrics/traces without capturing source or ROM contents (NFR-005–NFR-007).
  - [x] There is now one writer and one owner of the correlation identifier
    rather than six places that each formatted their own line. The identifier
    is taken from the `X-Correlation-ID` header the API already answered on,
    accepted only when it is of a shape that could not carry anything else —
    a caller-supplied string ends up in a log file, and an arbitrary one would
    let a caller write whatever they liked into the record, including lines
    that read like other records — and returned on every response so a person
    reporting a problem can quote the identifier the log lines carry.
  - [x] Redaction is by construction rather than by stripping. A value is
    logged only if it is of a shape that could not be content: a number, a
    boolean, or a short single-line printable-ASCII string that is not under a
    field name this service uses for content. Anything else is refused, and the
    refusal is written into the record by name and reason, because a log that
    silently dropped a field would be a log that lies about what happened. The
    judgement is one-sided on purpose: over-refusing costs a diagnostic
    message, and the other direction costs somebody their source.
  - [x] One record per request carrying method, route name, status, duration and
    body sizes — the measurements the service-level objectives are written
    against, and none of them a copy of anything. The route name is recorded
    rather than the path, because a path can carry a caller's own strings and a
    route name cannot. An unhandled exception records its class and location and
    never its message, since an exception message can quote the input that
    caused it and the input here is somebody's source.
  - [x] Evidence: 7 observability contracts plus one that runs a **real BeebAsm
    build** and searches the bytes the logger actually wrote for the label,
    mnemonics and directives of the source it was given. Asserting that the
    redactor was called would not have earned the claim.
- [x] PLAT-205 Integrate liveness/readiness and dependency/toolchain status into
  shared administration under scoped capabilities.
  - [x] Readiness now says why, not only whether. Each toolchain manifest builds
    its readiness from named checks — this executable, that version against the
    pinned one, this licence file — and derives the summary from them, so the
    summary cannot disagree with the detail. Every failure names what was
    examined and what to do about it; a pinned-version mismatch reports both
    numbers, because "wrong version" without saying which is not something
    anyone can act on. `/api/health/ready` gathers every unmet check across the
    four toolchains into one list.
  - [x] The workbench asks that endpoint and shows the answer. The readiness
    endpoint existed and nothing in the product read it, so a native build that
    failed for a missing toolchain reported only that it had failed, with the
    reason in a container log the person using the workbench cannot see. A
    service that does not answer at all is reported as not having answered
    rather than as not being ready, because those are different situations and
    only one of them is about a toolchain.
  - [x] The scoped-capability half is stated rather than implied: this is a
    service endpoint anyone who can reach the service can read, it is not an
    administrative surface, and nothing on it is restricted by role because this
    build has no accounts to restrict it to. That arrives with the deferred
    authentication work under CLD-800.
  - [x] Evidence: 6 readiness contracts, including one checked against a
    program that is certainly present in any environment the suite can run in
    at all, so the passing case is proved rather than assumed; plus 4 contracts
    on the status panel covering a ready service, an unmet check shown with the
    remedy the service gave for it, a service that did not answer being reported
    as not having answered, and the panel not implying that anything on it is
    restricted by role.
- [x] PLAT-206 Produce SBOM, dependency licence report, vulnerability scanning,
  pinned container/toolchain sources, and update policy.
  - [x] `docs/sbom.md` is generated from the lockfile, the built output and
    `npm audit`, because a hand-maintained inventory is out of date the first
    time anyone installs and nobody notices until it matters. `npm run sbom`
    regenerates it.
  - [x] What ships is decided on evidence rather than on the lockfile's
    development flag, and that distinction was not academic. A native image
    library arrives as a runtime dependency of the emulator core and brings
    sixteen LGPL platform binaries with it; none can be in a browser bundle and
    none is in the built output. Reporting those as distributed would have been
    sixteen licence obligations this product does not have. Of 546 installed
    packages, 75 are distributed, 195 are development-only, and 276 are
    installed but not distributed with the reason recorded against each, because
    an exclusion nobody can check is one nobody should trust. The judgement is
    deliberately one-sided: anything not proven undistributable stays counted as
    distributed, so an unproven case over-reports rather than misses.
  - [x] Licence classification handles the expressions npm actually publishes.
    An OR expression is as permissive as its most permissive arm; an AND
    expression binds every arm at once and is copyleft the moment one arm is —
    treating `Apache-2.0 AND LGPL-3.0-or-later AND MIT` as permissive because
    one arm is MIT would drop a real obligation. The `-only` and `-or-later`
    spellings are recognised, since those are what the registry carries. An
    expression it does not recognise is reported as unrecognised rather than
    assumed safe, and a missing licence as undetermined rather than as none,
    because those are different statements and only one is a problem to resolve.
  - [x] **One shipped dependency carries a copyleft obligation: jsbeeb 1.19.1 is
    GPL-3.0-or-later and is present in the built output.** That is a real
    constraint on distributing the workbench bundle and sits alongside the
    GPL-2.0 position already recorded for the vendored ElkJS core. It is named
    in the report rather than counted, and is for the deferred licence review
    under P0-018 to settle.
  - [x] `npm audit` currently reports no vulnerability at any severity, and the
    report carries the counts rather than the claim. Every installed package has
    a verifiable integrity hash. Pinned container and toolchain sources are
    recorded in `docs/third-party-components.md` with checksums the release gate
    verifies, and the update policy is in `docs/security-and-privacy.md`.
  - [x] Evidence: 15 contracts covering every licence class, both connective
    forms, the -only and -or-later spellings, undetermined against none,
    unrecognised expressions not being assumed safe, each exclusion rule,
    evidence of presence overriding an exclusion, an ordinary package staying
    counted as distributed, the counts adding up, every package having an
    integrity hash, every shipped copyleft dependency being named including
    jsbeeb, the vendored cores being declared out of scope of the lockfile, and
    determinism.

### 4.2 Profiles, configuration, and manifests

- [x] PRF-200 Implement machine/expansion profile registry, schema validation,
  migration, capability negotiation, and stable IDs (TGT-001–TGT-009).
  - [x] The shipped catalogue is validated rather than trusted, by a contract
    test that names each problem and where it is: a duplicate machine or ROM
    identifier, an unregistered platform class, a missing label or description,
    an accent that is not a colour, no variants or no ROM profiles at all, a
    capability state that is not supported, preview or planned, a planned
    capability that is nonetheless enabled by default, and a peripheral fitted
    to a variant the machine does not list.
  - [x] Capability negotiation is explicit. A capability that is fitted to a
    particular variant now names that variant in a field resolution can check,
    rather than describing it in prose nobody could enforce. Adding the field
    immediately exposed two real errors: the Atom's AtomDOS capability required
    an "AtomDOS variant" that did not exist under that name, and AtoMMC could be
    enabled on a plain Atom 12K, claiming a mass-storage interface that is not
    fitted. Both now resolve correctly and the control is disabled with the
    reason on it.
  - [x] Profile schema versioning and migration now exist for manifests that
    arrive from outside this build. The shipped catalogue is a literal checked
    by a contract and cannot be wrong at run time; a manifest is the other case
    — written elsewhere, exported by an older build, or carried in a project —
    and none of it can be assumed. A manifest declares its version, every
    version this product has written stays readable, and one from a newer build
    is refused by name rather than parsed as though the fields it lacks were
    simply absent.
  - [x] A field the manifest's own version predates is absent rather than
    missing. Version 2 added a capability's variant restriction, so a version 1
    capability is read as unrestricted, which is exactly what version 1 meant;
    carrying such a field forward from a document that could not express it
    would invent a restriction nobody wrote.
  - [x] Anything this build cannot use is dropped and named, never guessed at.
    A capability whose state is not one this product defines is dropped rather
    than defaulted, because defaulting to supported would claim hardware and
    defaulting to planned would silently remove a capability the machine has —
    neither is this build's decision. An unknown platform class is reported with
    the profile still usable and marked as needing a check, because a partly
    usable profile beats a refused one provided nothing was invented in silence.
    A malformed accent is corrected rather than refused, since nobody should
    lose a machine over a colour.
  - [x] Evidence: 17 manifest contracts covering a round trip of every shipped
    profile with nothing changed and nothing reported, the version this build
    writes, a version 1 migration and its unrestricted capability, a version 2
    restriction kept, the newer-build refusal by name, four malformed version
    values, four documents that are not manifests at all, a capability state
    dropped rather than guessed, capabilities and ROM profiles with no identity
    dropped and named, an unknown platform class reported as needing a check,
    a profile with no usable variant kept selectable, a duplicate variant
    dropped, a corrected accent, the prose saying why a capability is not
    fitted being carried through, and every change reported as one readable
    line.
- [x] PRF-201 Implement deterministic configuration resolver with conflict/
  dependency explanations and immutable resolved manifest.
  - [x] Resolution used to substitute in silence: a project asking for a
    machine, variant or ROM this build does not have was given the first entry
    in the list, and capabilities it asked for were dropped without a word. That
    meant opening someone else's project could hand you a different computer
    than the one they wrote for with no indication anything had changed.
  - [x] It still substitutes, because a workbench has to show something, but
    every departure is now returned as a diagnostic naming what was requested,
    what is in effect, and why, in one of eight kinds: an unregistered platform
    class, an unknown machine, a machine belonging to another platform class, an
    unknown variant, an unknown firmware profile, an unknown capability, a
    planned capability, and a peripheral fitted to another variant. The target
    selector shows them where the machine is chosen.
  - [x] Evidence: a headless run opened a project asking for an Atom with a
    firmware profile called `kernel`, the AtoMMC interface and a
    `teletext-adapter` capability. The workbench reported `1 substitution · 2
    requests not applied`, named the missing ROM profile and what it used
    instead, said AtoMMC is fitted to the `Atom 12K + AtoMMC` variant while
    `Atom 12K` was selected, said the Atom has no `teletext-adapter` capability,
    and disabled the AtoMMC control with that reason on it. Zero console errors.
- [x] PRF-202 Implement target selector and support-state/legal-ROM displays.
  - [x] The selector already showed each capability's support state and the
    firmware vault already showed the adapter support matrix. It now also shows
    every departure of the configuration in effect from the one requested, so a
    substitution is visible in the place the machine is chosen rather than only
    in a notice that scrolls away.
  - [x] Evidence: Covered by the profile-registry contracts, which prove an
   unknown machine, variant or ROM is reported as a diagnostic rather than
   silently substituted, and by the compatibility matrix contracts, which
   prove no machine is ever presented as runnable without an engine behind it.
- [x] PRF-203 Implement configuration editor for RAM, ROM/sideways slots,
  firmware, storage, input, and supported expansions.
  - [x] The target configuration panel already carried platform class, machine,
    the model-and-memory variant, the firmware set, a hardware summary of CPU,
    memory, ROM note and firmware readiness, and a capability list covering
    storage, input and every supported expansion — each with its state, the
    prose saying what a planned one still needs, and the control disabled with
    the reason when a peripheral belongs to a variant that is not selected.
    Fitted memory is shown and not editable, which is correct: it is a property
    of the variant rather than a setting.
  - [x] Sideways banks were the real gap, and the reason they matter is why.
    Mounting worked by derivation — enable a capability and the ROM it needs was
    mounted somewhere — which is enough to run a machine and not enough to
    develop for one. A sideways ROM's bank decides its service-call priority, so
    it decides which ROM answers a star command first and which wins when two
    claim the same name, and none of that is visible if the product picks the
    number. A bank is now something a person assigns.
  - [x] All sixteen banks are shown, filled or not, because an empty bank is
    information: it is where the next ROM goes and its number is what that ROM's
    priority will be. Sixteen is stated as architectural — the ROM select
    register is four bits wide — while which banks a particular machine has
    fitted and what its own firmware occupies is taken from the ROM set
    definition rather than assumed, because this build will not claim that BASIC
    lives in a particular bank on a particular model unless its own data says so.
  - [x] A refusal never relocates an image. Choosing a different bank on
    someone's behalf would change the priority they were setting, which is the
    one thing the editor exists to let them control, so an occupied bank, a bank
    the machine's firmware holds, a bank the hardware does not have and the same
    image in two banks are each refused by name with what to do instead. An
    image is checked before it is placed, using the same validator the firmware
    vault uses, so one accepted here is accepted there; a combined multi-bank
    dump is told to be split rather than silently truncated. The service-call
    order is stated once more than one ROM is fitted. ROM images stay in the
    browser and are never written into the project or uploaded.
  - [x] Evidence: 20 slot contracts covering the bank count and its reason,
    every valid and invalid bank number, all sixteen rows shown, each refusal
    including the promise that nothing is moved, clearing a bank and the refusal
    to clear a reserved one, image validation for the exact size, a combined
    image, a blank image and a good header, a layout arriving from elsewhere
    with a duplicated bank, a duplicated image or an impossible bank number, and
    the service-call order being highest bank first without mutating its input.
    Ten panel contracts drive the real editor: every bank shown, the stated
    reason the number matters, a placement, a refused combined image, a filled
    bank not offered again, emptying a bank, no way to empty a reserved one, the
    service-call order on screen, a machine with no sideways ROM saying so
    rather than showing an empty table, and the statement that images are never
    uploaded.
- [x] PRF-204 Implement profile comparison and portability warnings.
  - [x] `compareConfigurations` reports what differs between two resolved
    configurations and what would not survive a move between them: a different
    processor family means compiled code will not transfer, different fitted
    memory means a fixed load address may not fit, different firmware means
    operating-system entry points and workspace addresses are not guaranteed to
    match, and a capability the work depends on is distinguished between one the
    target machine merely has switched off and one it does not have at all.
  - [x] The warnings are raised where they matter: opening a project reports
    both the resolution diagnostics for its stored target and, when that target
    resolves exactly, what changes relative to the configuration in effect.
  - [x] A side-by-side comparison view now answers the question the warnings
    cannot. The warnings are raised when a project is opened, which is the right
    moment to be told and the wrong moment to be deciding: by then the choice is
    made and the person is reading about something they cannot easily undo. Any
    two configurations this build knows can now be compared before choosing,
    with the machine, variant and firmware selectable on each side and only the
    variants and firmware the chosen machine actually has offered.
  - [x] It shows the same answer the warnings show, from the same comparison,
    because two surfaces answering one question differently would be worse than
    one — and it says so on the panel. Where nothing differs it says so rather
    than showing an empty table, and where the differences are real but none
    stops work moving it says that too, because a panel that always finds
    something to warn about is one nobody reads. Anything the build had to
    change to resolve either configuration is listed alongside.
  - [x] Evidence: 8 panel contracts covering every registered machine offered on
    both sides, identical configurations reported as identical, real differences
    named, the warning that compiled code will not transfer between processor
    families, no invented warning when work still moves, variants and firmware
    re-offered from the chosen machine, the readable summary of each side, and
    the statement that this is the same comparison the project-open warning
    raises.
- [x] PRF-205 Contract-test invalid, unknown, migrated, conflicting, and
  extension-bearing manifests.
  - [x] 19 contracts cover the invalid, the unknown and the conflicting: a
    catalogue with duplicate identifiers, a malformed accent, no variants and no
    ROM profiles; a machine, variant, firmware profile, platform class and
    capability the build does not have; a machine that exists on another
    platform class; a peripheral fitted to a variant that is not selected; and a
    request in which every field is wrong at once, which must still produce a
    usable target.
  - [x] Migrated manifests are now covered by the 17 manifest contracts
    recorded under PRF-200: a version 1 document migrated forward, the field
    version 2 added being absent rather than invented on the way, a version 2
    document keeping it, and a document from a newer version refused by name.
  - [x] Extension-bearing manifests are covered by the same set. A manifest
    carrying a capability, ROM profile or platform class this build does not
    know is read for what it can use and reports the rest, rather than being
    refused whole or having values invented for it.

### 4.3 Local project and settings

Current implemented increment (the broader tickets below remain open):

- [x] Browser-local versioned project document with new/open/save, recovery,
  portable JSON export, source import, and filename collision handling.
- [x] Working source tree and multi-file tab operations: create, select, edit,
  rename, delete, download, modified-state display, and explicit save.
- [x] Version-two project document persists resolved target selection and source
  breakpoints, with tested migration from version one.

- [x] PRJ-199 Ship worked sample projects and a create-from-existing-codebase
  path so a new user reaches a building, running, testable project without
  authoring one first.
  - [x] Two complete sample games ship as portable project documents and open
    through the ordinary project parser, so they are validated and migrated
    exactly like an imported project and are editable in every respect. Acorn
    Harvest is a BBC Model B MODE 5 maze game in 6502 assembly across seven
    modules with three `INCLUDEASSET` pixel assets, an animated player sprite
    read through its generated frame table, a tile map, decimal-mode scoring,
    OSWORD 7 sound, project bookmarks and a second self-test build target with
    a real-machine test plan. Acorn Catcher is a structured BBC BASIC II game
    with `VDU 23` characters, an `ENVELOPE` note and one `DEF PROC` per job.
  - [x] Evidence: 16 sample contracts prove both samples parse, that every
    build target validates against the sample machine, that every target builds
    with no diagnostics and byte-identically on repeat, that every test plan
    resolves against its own build symbols, and that each pixel asset generates
    deterministic output. Three further contracts prove the self test asserts
    the same first sprite byte the asset pipeline actually packs, the screen
    addresses the MODE 5 layout actually produces, and an acorn total that
    matches the level data shipped beside it. A headed Chromium run from a
    clean profile opened the catalogue, opened Acorn Harvest through the real
    dialog with all eleven files, and ran Test all on a genuine BBC Model B
    with locally supplied ROMs: 1/1 passed, zero console errors.
  - [x] Create a project from an existing folder of source. The plan is shown
    in full before anything is created: files with their language, exclusions
    with reasons, renames forced by flat project filenames, inferred build
    targets with the evidence for each choice, pixel assets recoverable from
    existing `EQUB` data, and map-shaped data reported without a document being
    invented for it. A recovered asset is offered only when unpacking and
    repacking reproduces the original bytes exactly, and the source it came
    from is never modified. Folders are read in the browser and never uploaded.
  - [x] Evidence: 16 importer contracts, 12 asset-recovery contracts and 7
    dialog contracts, including round-tripping every byte value through both
    packings, the file-count, file-size and project-size limits, undecodable
    text, ignored directories, and a full re-import of the Acorn Harvest tree
    that still assembles with no diagnostics. A headed Chromium run drove the
    real folder picker: 4 files planned, 3 excluded, 2 build targets inferred
    with reasons, both recoverable assets listed with the tile-map overlap
    warning, and the project created with the selected asset and zero console
    errors.
  - [x] A folder opened through the File System Access API keeps its handle for
    the session, so the project can be written back into the folder it came
    from rather than only downloaded. The browsers without the API — Firefox
    and Safari at the time of writing — are told plainly what they cannot do
    instead of being offered a control that would fail, and the one-way
    directory input remains for them. Writing asks for write permission at the
    point of saving, never at import; a refusal writes nothing at all. Only
    authored and imported sources are written, never generated output. A path
    ending in a separator, or one containing `..`, is refused rather than
    resolved into somewhere the person did not choose. A handle cannot be
    persisted, so a reload honestly loses the connection and the command
    reports itself unavailable rather than appearing to work.
  - [x] Evidence: 16 directory contracts covering the walk, the ignored build
    directories, non-text files, the file-count and byte ceilings, an
    unreadable file, a refused permission, a partial write, and both refused
    path shapes; and 4 further dialog contracts proving a browser without the
    picker is told so, that the handle reaches the workbench, that a one-way
    import claims no connection, and that an empty folder is reported rather
    than opened as a project.
  - [x] The inferred entry point of every proposed build target can be changed
    before the project is created. Each target lists the files of its own
    language, best first, with what was found in each; choosing one re-derives
    the target's name and output from it, so a build never names a different
    file from the one it assembles. A file of another language, or one not in
    the import, is refused rather than silently substituted. Reported tile-map
    data is promoted into a real editable map document at a chosen grid shape,
    with the source it came from left unmodified.
  - [x] Evidence: 6 override contracts covering the candidate list per
    language, the re-derived name and output, the toolchain's own output
    extension, all three refusals, the chosen entry reaching the created
    project, and an unchanged plan being returned unchanged; plus a dialog
    contract that drives the real selector and checks the created project's
    entry file, and the existing contract that promotes level data to a map
    document and parses it back at the chosen shape. A headless Chromium run
    against the built artefact drove the whole round trip: the write-back
    command listed and disabled before any folder was connected, a folder
    opened through the real dialog and reported as connected, both 6502 entry
    candidates offered with `main.asm` inferred, the entry overridden to
    `engine.asm` through the real selector with the reason shown, the created
    project's build target naming `engine.asm` and producing `engine.bin`, the
    command then enabled, and all three sources written back into the same
    folder with their content intact — zero console errors.
- [x] PRJ-200 Finalize versioned project and portable bundle schemas (PRJ-001).
  - [x] Which project formats are accepted is now derived from a single version
    number rather than restated as two hand-maintained lists of every version
    string. Those lists were a drift hazard in the only direction that matters:
    bumping the format meant remembering to extend both, and a missed entry
    would have made the product refuse a document it had itself written. The
    version at which files began carrying a saved baseline is likewise a
    number, not a list.
  - [x] A document from a newer build is refused as a newer document, naming
    the format it carries and the highest this build reads, rather than being
    reported as unsupported. The same holds for a portable bundle. "Update the
    workbench" is actionable; "not supported" invites someone to edit the
    version field, which would then fail the digest check for the wrong reason.
  - [x] Evidence: a contract that opens a document of every format version this
    product has ever written, from 1 to the current one, and checks each
    migrates to the current format with its files intact and no false unsaved
    state; a contract on the version parser covering leading zeros, a zero
    version, a non-numeric suffix, a different schema prefix and a non-string;
    both newer-document refusals; and a whole-document round trip that
    populates every section — breakpoints, bookmarks, both breakpoint models
    with their groups, analysis annotations and project settings — serialises,
    reopens and compares the documents whole, so a field added to the model
    without parser support fails here rather than being discovered as data
    lost from someone's project. A second contract compares the document's key
    set before and after, so a whole section cannot go missing.
- [x] PRJ-201 Implement in-browser/local project repository with safe normalized
  paths, transactions, quotas, crash recovery, and corruption handling.
  - [x] Safe names. Project filenames were only checked for slashes, which was
    survivable while a project left as a download and stopped being survivable
    once one could be written back into a folder on disk: the names in the
    project become names on a real filesystem. One rule now decides, shared by
    the project model and the disk writer, so a name a project accepts is a
    name that can be written. It folds path separators, removes the characters
    and control codes no common filesystem accepts, refuses `.` and `..` as
    filenames, drops a trailing dot or space that Windows would discard
    silently — turning two distinct names into one file — renames the
    twenty-two Windows device names, and caps length while keeping the
    extension so the language is still known. A name that had to change is
    reported with the reason when a file is created or renamed, because
    silently altering what someone typed is how they end up looking for a file
    that is not there.
  - [x] Transactions. A save now reads back what it wrote and compares it. A
    browser can accept a write and store nothing, or store part of it, without
    throwing anything for the quota branch to catch — a private window near its
    limit, or storage partitioned by an extension — and a save reported as
    successful that did not happen is the one failure that costs someone their
    work. A mismatch is undone, restoring the previous snapshot, and reported
    with both byte counts.
  - [x] Quotas. What this origin is holding is measured and named in the
    full-storage message. No limit is reported, because the browser does not
    expose one and a figure invented here would be wrong somewhere and would be
    believed.
  - [x] Crash recovery and corruption were already covered: the project
    autosaves, a snapshot that cannot be read is moved aside intact rather than
    replaced, and the preserved bytes can be downloaded.
  - [x] Evidence: 13 naming contracts covering ordinary names left untouched,
    separators, refused characters and control codes, both directory names, a
    kept leading dot, trailing dots and spaces, all twenty-two device names and
    the names that merely resemble them, length capping, a usable name from
    every degenerate input, and idempotence — a name the rule produced is never
    changed again; 8 write-path contracts; and 6 storage contracts covering a
    write the browser did not keep with and without a previous save, a
    truncated write with its rollback, the measured usage, an unreadable store
    reporting nothing rather than zero, and the usage figure appearing in the
    quota message.
- [x] PRJ-202 Implement project tree operations, undo/trash, drag and keyboard
  alternatives, authored/imported/SDK/generated/build groups (PRJ-003–PRJ-005).
  Sources are grouped as authored, imported and generated. A separate SDK group
  is not carried: nothing in the product marks a file as coming from an SDK, and
  inventing the distinction would mean asserting an origin the data does not
  record.
  - [x] Deleting a file was a confirmation and then it was gone, along with
    every build target that named it and every bookmark inside it. A
    confirmation is not an undo: it asks whether you meant it, which is a
    different question from whether you were right. A deleted file now goes to
    the project's own trash together with what was removed alongside it, so
    restoring puts back the state that existed rather than an approximation.
    The trash lives in the project, so it survives a reload and travels with an
    export, and is bounded at 25 entries with anything dropped reported.
  - [x] Restoring resolves the two ways the project can have moved on: a name
    taken since the deletion causes a rename rather than an overwrite, and a
    build-target identifier since reused is reported as not restored rather than
    replacing whatever now holds it. Trashing the last file, or one that is not
    there, is refused with the reason.
  - [x] The tree is grouped by where a file came from, because that governs what
    may be done to it: authored, imported and generated, each with a count, then
    the build targets and their artifacts, then the trash.
  - [x] It held one tab stop per file, so a project with fifty sources put fifty
    stops between the explorer and the editor. It now holds a single stop and
    moves between rows with the arrow keys, Home and End, across group
    boundaries, with focus following so a screen reader announces the row.
  - [x] The tree was extracted from `App.tsx` into its own component so this
    behaviour is directly testable; 13 contracts cover the grouping, the single
    tab stop, the movement, the selection reporting and the trash controls.
  - [x] Evidence in the built workbench: the tree reported its groups and
    exactly one tab stop across five rows; deleting an imported file left two
    files and one trash entry named `notes.txt`; the trash row offered Restore
    and Delete; restoring returned the project to three files. Zero console
    errors.
  - [x] Files reorder by dragging, with the row that is moving marked and a line
    showing which side of the target it would land on. The keyboard alternative
    is Alt with an arrow, which performs exactly the same move; reordering is
    not offered as a pointer-only affordance. Every move is announced with the
    new position, so a drag that changed nothing says so.
  - [x] A drop across a group boundary is refused with the reason. The groups
    are read from each file's recorded origin, so moving a file into another
    group would either do nothing visible or imply its origin had changed;
    saying `notes.txt is imported and game.asm is authored` is the honest
    answer. Dragging also carries the file name as plain text, so a drag out of
    the tree into another application means something.
  - [x] Holding the drag identity only in React state left a real defect: a drag
    that reached its first `dragover` in the same frame as `dragstart` found no
    drag in progress and refused the drop. It is now held in a ref as well, and
    a contract exercises exactly that ordering.
  - [x] Evidence in the built workbench, through real drag events: dragging
    `game.asm` below `loader.asm` reordered the stored project to `loader.asm,
    game.asm, notes.txt` and reported `game.asm moved to position 2 of 3`;
    dragging the imported `notes.txt` onto an authored file was refused with the
    group explanation and left the order unchanged.
- [x] PRJ-203 Implement file/folder/archive import preview with duplicate,
  traversal, symlink, decompression, encoding, and quota handling.
  - [x] A zip archive is now a third way source arrives, alongside the
    directory input and the File System Access API, and it reaches the same
    import plan as the other two so a person sees the same preview whichever
    route the source took. Acorn source is passed around as archives — a forum
    attachment, a release, a backup of a working directory — and until now none
    of that could be opened without unpacking it by hand first.
  - [x] An archive is hostile input, and every claim in one is checked rather
    than believed. A name that is absolute or contains `..` is refused by name,
    because unpacking it naively writes outside the project. A symbolic link is
    a path stored where content should be, so it is reported as a link rather
    than followed. An encrypted entry and a compression method this reader does
    not implement are each named rather than producing rubbish. The declared
    uncompressed size is bounded before anything is expanded, and — because a
    header can simply lie — decompression stops at the bound rather than after
    it, so the memory a hostile archive can make the tab allocate is the limit
    and not whatever the archive felt like producing. Every entry's CRC-32 is
    checked against the archive's own record, which catches a truncated entry,
    an altered one, and a header that disagrees with its data. Anything that is
    not UTF-8 text is reported rather than imported as source. A zip64 archive
    is refused outright rather than half-read through the 32-bit fields it
    deliberately overflows. Nothing is uploaded: the platform's own
    `DecompressionStream` does the work, so there is no dependency to audit.
  - [x] The reader is central-directory-first, and takes each entry's data
    offset from its own local header rather than assuming the two extra fields
    match — they routinely do not, and assuming otherwise reads from the wrong
    place.
  - [x] Duplicates, encoding and quota were already handled by the importer the
    archive feeds: flat-name collisions are renamed and reported, undecodable
    files are excluded with the reason, and the per-file, file-count and
    project-total limits apply to an archive exactly as to a folder.
  - [x] Evidence: 18 archive contracts, every one driving a real zip built byte
    by byte in the test and deflated by the platform's own CompressionStream —
    stored and deflated entries, a trailing archive comment, backslash names,
    the ignored build directories, all four escaping name shapes, a symbolic
    link and an ordinary Windows file that must not be mistaken for one, an
    encrypted entry, an unimplemented method, an over-declared size, a
    decompression bomb whose header claims four bytes, a checksum mismatch,
    binary and invalid-UTF-8 content, zip64, a file that is not an archive, and
    a truncated one; plus the published CRC-32 check value. Three dialog
    contracts drive the real control with a real archive: the plan and the
    named refusals, an archive holding nothing importable, and a file that is
    not an archive at all.
- [x] PRJ-204 Implement complete bundle export/import with integrity manifest,
  migrations, dependencies, and excluded-ROM/secrets report.
  - [x] Exporting was a plain JSON dump: enough to move work between two copies
    of the same build, and nothing more. It did not say what the project needs
    in order to build, did not say what had been left out, and could not tell a
    truncated or edited file from a good one. It is now a versioned
    `8bit-net.project-bundle`.
  - [x] An integrity manifest carries a digest of the project in a canonical
    form plus a digest and byte count for every file. Opening verifies both
    before anything is parsed, so a bundle altered in transit is refused with
    what no longer matches rather than opened as if it were what its author
    sent, and a migration never runs over contents that were not.
  - [x] A dependency report names the machine profile, variant, firmware set,
    capabilities and toolchains the work expects, and names any source a build
    target references that the bundle does not contain, so the receiver learns
    that before discovering it by failing to build.
  - [x] An exclusion report states what deliberately did not travel and why:
    ROM and firmware images, build artifacts, browser settings, test history,
    and private bookmarks when they were held back.
  - [x] Source is scanned for private keys, authorization headers, assigned
    passwords, connection strings and cloud access keys. Findings are reported
    with the file and line, and with the value masked so the report never
    repeats it, before a file is produced. They are reported, not removed: the
    author decides. The patterns are deliberately narrow, and a contract asserts
    they stay silent on ordinary Acorn assembly full of hexadecimal constants.
  - [x] Evidence: 17 unit contracts plus a headless run of the real interface.
    The export dialog listed what the bundle needs, the four things it leaves
    out, and `game.asm:2 · assigned password` before anything was written.
    Downloading produced a bundle with a project digest and two file digests.
    Feeding an altered copy back through the real Open project input was refused
    with `This bundle has been altered since it was created: the project digest
    is efdbbb58… not the 5eac5ad5… it records`, and the open project was
    untouched. The intact bundle then opened, reporting `bundle verified against
    its manifest · 2 files · 2 build targets · 1 toolchain`. Zero console
    errors.
- [x] PRJ-205 Implement layered default/user/workspace/project settings with
  import/export/reset, schema validation, and unknown-setting preservation.
  - [x] Preferences were previously a browser-storage key each, read and written
    wherever they happened to be used, with no schema and no way to move a setup
    between browsers. They now resolve through three layers in increasing
    precedence: what this build ships, what the person chose in this browser,
    and what the open project carries. A project can therefore carry a
    preference the work needs without changing the person's own default.
  - [x] A value that fails its schema is not coerced. The layer is skipped, the
    layer below answers, and the settings surface says which layer was ignored
    and why. Writing an invalid value is refused with the same reason rather
    than stored and discovered later at the point of use.
  - [x] The registry takes its value sets from the modules that own them —
    the scale modes, display effects, runtime speeds, volume and the key-remap
    and gamepad validators — so a setting can never offer something the runtime
    would refuse. A contract test compares the two, and caught exactly that:
    the first draft offered runtime speeds of 1, 2, 4 and 8 where the machine
    accepts 0.5, 1, 2 and 4.
  - [x] Export, import and reset. Export writes a versioned `8bit-net.settings`
    document of what has actually been set. Import validates each entry on its
    own, so one bad value does not discard the rest, and reports what was
    applied, what was refused and why. Reset clears only the settings that were
    set and names them.
  - [x] Unknown settings are preserved. A storage entry this build has never
    heard of is carried through export and written back on import, so a document
    written by a newer build survives a round trip through an older one.
  - [x] Projects, firmware, test history and asset drafts are explicitly not
    settings: they are excluded from export, and an import cannot write one back
    through the unknown-entry channel, which is contract-tested.
  - [x] Project settings persist at format `8bit-net-dev-project-20`, validated
    against the same schema on the way in, so an imported project cannot
    introduce a preference the machine would refuse.
  - [x] 20 contracts cover the layering, the refusals, the documents and the
    schema-against-runtime comparison.
  - [x] Evidence in the built workbench: with a project carrying a volume of 70,
    a browser value of 35 and a stored emulator scale of `enormous`, the
    settings table reported the volume as 70 from This project, the scale as fit
    from Built in, and said `The value stored in this browser was ignored:
    "enormous" must be one of fit, 1x, 2x.` Exporting wrote the browser value
    and the unrecognised `future-preference` entry but neither the corrupt value
    nor the project. Carrying the framebuffer filter with the project through
    the interface wrote it into the stored project at format
    `8bit-net-dev-project-20`. Zero console errors.
- [x] PRJ-206 Implement dirty state, autosave, explicit save, recovery, storage
  status, and unload/crash tests (PRJ-008).
  - [x] Autosave was a bare `setItem` with nothing around it, which gave the
    product two ways to mislead. A full quota throws: the write failed, the
    interface said nothing, and the next reload would have lost everything since
    the last good save while the session looked untouched. Saving now returns
    its outcome, a failure is shown in the status line and as a notice, and the
    previous snapshot is left exactly as it was so the fallback is the last good
    save rather than nothing.
  - [x] A stored project that would not parse was replaced with a new empty one.
    From the outside that is indistinguishable from the product having thrown
    the work away, and the bytes were sitting in storage the whole time. They
    are now moved aside intact, the reason is reported with the parse error and
    the byte count, and they can be downloaded. Nothing is deleted until the
    person chooses to.
  - [x] The status line carries the save state, reading `Saved` or `NOT SAVED`,
    with the time and size of the last successful save in its title.
  - [x] Storage that is unavailable entirely — blocked by policy or a private
    window — is reported as unavailable rather than as an empty project.
  - [x] 11 unit contracts cover the outcomes, including a simulated
    `QuotaExceededError` proving the previous snapshot survives, a snapshot that
    is valid JSON but not a project, and storage that throws on read.
  - [x] Evidence in the built workbench: a deliberately truncated snapshot was
    written before the page's own scripts ran. The workbench reported `The saved
    project could not be read: Unterminated string in JSON at position 120. Its
    120 bytes have been kept aside and can be downloaded rather than discarded`,
    kept all 120 bytes in storage, offered them in Settings, downloaded a file
    byte-identical to what was preserved, and removed them only when Discard was
    chosen. Zero console errors.
- [x] PRJ-207 Create target-aware template catalogue schema and two lawful
  templates for the first machine slice.
  - [x] The samples are finished programs — the right thing for reading, the
    wrong thing for starting, because someone who wants to write their own game
    has to delete a maze first. A template is the other half: a small,
    complete, buildable skeleton with the machine it was written for recorded
    beside it.
  - [x] Target-awareness is the schema rather than a label on it. A template
    declares the machine, variant, ROM set and the capabilities it needs, and
    the catalogue reports whether the configuration in front of you actually
    offers them. A capability the machine lists as planned is not a capability:
    the product says elsewhere that planned means not fitted, so a template
    depending on one is refused rather than offered and then found to fail. A
    template is offered, or it is listed with the reason it cannot be — never
    offered and broken.
  - [x] Two templates for the BBC Model B. A MODE 7 starter in 6502 assembly
    that selects the teletext screen through VDU 22, prints a zero-terminated
    string through OSWRCH with a zero-page pointer, waits on OSRDCH and returns
    to BASIC rather than hanging, staying inside the &70 workspace the OS
    reserves for user programs. A disk catalogue starter in BASIC II that reads
    a file's load address, execution address and length through OSFILE with
    A%=5, without loading the file — the starting point for anything that keeps
    its data on disk. The second requires the DFS capability and says so; the
    first requires nothing beyond the base machine and runs on a cassette
    Model B too.
  - [x] Both are original to this product and each carries its provenance —
    author, licence and a sentence on what it does and does not derive from —
    so the licence review at the end of this project has a statement to check
    rather than an assumption to make. They call documented OS entry points,
    which is an interface rather than a work; nothing is taken from Acorn
    firmware or from a published listing.
  - [x] Writing the first template exposed a real gap in the browser-local
    assembler: it had no `NAME = expr` constants at all. BeebAsm accepts them
    and real Acorn source is full of them, so any project imported from a
    BeebAsm tree assembled everywhere except here. Constants are now supported,
