- [ ] CLD-801 Implement backend-enforced owner/editor/tester/viewer project
  capabilities and shared-admin service/operator capabilities.
- [ ] CLD-802 Implement encrypted/isolated project metadata and blob storage,
  integrity checks, quotas, transactional revision references, and safe GC.
- [ ] CLD-803 Implement explicit local/cloud modes and migration without account
  coercion or local data loss (CLD-001–CLD-002).
- [ ] CLD-804 Implement sync state machine, offline queue, reconnect, text merge,
  manifest/asset conflict resolution, and fork fallback.
- [ ] CLD-805 Implement revision timeline, compare, restore, fork, actor/source,
  retention, large-binary dedupe, and immutable shared revisions.
- [ ] CLD-806 Implement share/invite/revoke/public template controls with secret,
  ROM, licence, and redistribution scanning.
- [ ] CLD-807 Implement quota dashboard and predictable cache/artifact/revision
  eviction/deletion with warnings.
- [ ] CLD-808 Implement user/project export and deletion with tested retention,
  tombstone, backup, and audit behavior.
- [ ] CLD-809 Pen-test cross-tenant access, object identifiers, invitations,
  public links, revisions, builds, debug sessions, exports, and WebSockets.

### Phase 8 exit gate

- [ ] CLOUD-GATE The entire core remains usable locally; authenticated users can
  sync, revise, compare, restore, share, export, and delete within tested scoped
  permissions, conflict rules, quotas, and privacy policy.

## 11. Phase 9 — hardening, operations, and release

### 11.1 Reliability and performance

- [ ] OPS-900 Implement dashboards and alerts for every NFR-007 metric with
  tenant-safe drill-down and runbooks.
- [ ] OPS-901 Load-test API, job queues, sandboxes, object storage, WebSockets,
  trace streaming, cloud sync, and reference search to accepted SLO/capacity.
- [ ] OPS-902 Benchmark application startup, edit latency, live diagnostics,
  build, emulator input/frame/audio, debugger acknowledgement, trace, and asset
  canvases across supported browsers/hardware classes.
- [x] OPS-903 Enforce and exercise size/concurrency/retention limits with clear
  user errors and no partial corruption.
  - [x] The limits were already enforced, each in the module that owns it. What
    did not exist was anywhere a person could see them, and that matters more
    than it sounds: a limit you only meet by exceeding it is indistinguishable
    from a bug, so the first thing someone does when an import stops half way
    is assume the product is broken rather than that it told them something.
    Twenty-six limits are now registered in one place and shown in Settings,
    grouped as size, count, retention and concurrency.
  - [x] The register holds no numbers of its own. Every value is imported from
    the module that enforces it, so it cannot say one thing while the code does
    another, and a contract checks each against its owner. Every entry states
    why the limit exists — a number with no reason is a number nobody can argue
    with later, and limits are exactly what needs arguing with when a real
    project turns out bigger than someone assumed — and what happens on
    reaching it. An entry that says only that it fails is refused by the
    validator, because that leaves a person nothing to act on.
  - [x] Each limit is exercised at its boundary rather than described: the
    value at the limit is accepted and one past it is refused with something
    actionable, while nothing is left half applied. A filename at exactly the
    limit is untouched and one past it is shortened keeping its extension; a
    source file of exactly the maximum size opens and one byte more is refused
    with the project unchanged; an import past the file count says it stopped
    and how many it read; the trash keeps the newest entries and names the ones
    it dropped, because a trash that quietly forgets is worse than none; an
    archive entry whose header claims four bytes and whose stream produces a
    megabyte is stopped at the bound; and asking for more concurrent builds
    than the ceiling does not raise it.
  - [x] Evidence: 12 register and boundary contracts, and 5 panel contracts
    driving the real Settings surface — every limit shown and grouped, each row
    carrying its value, reason and behaviour, byte values written in the unit a
    person reads, search narrowing and reporting when nothing matches, and the
    stated promise that nothing is truncated silently.
- [x] OPS-904 Test cancellation, worker/browser crash, dependency outage,
  reconnect, stale job, duplicate event, backpressure, quota race, and disk-full
  recovery.
  - [x] Every failure here is one the happy path never reaches, and that a
    person therefore meets for the first time when things are already going
    badly. The property asserted throughout is not that the product recovers.
    It is that it says what happened and leaves nothing half applied, because a
    silent failure and a partial write are both worse than an error: both look
    like success.
  - [x] A worker that dies is covered three ways — a crash carrying a message,
    a crash carrying none, which is the common case for a module worker that
    failed to parse and where an empty error tells nobody anything, and a worker
    that answers with a failure and no diagnostic at all. In each the task
    rejects with something readable and the worker is terminated rather than
    left running.
  - [x] A result arriving when it is no longer wanted is covered three ways: an
    identifier from an earlier request is ignored, which prevents the worst kind
    of failure — an answer that is real and about the wrong question; a second,
    contradictory delivery of the same request settles nothing new; and anything
    arriving after a cancellation is dropped.
  - [x] The same question for language answers, which are asynchronous while the
    document keeps changing underneath them. A revision captured during a
    request stops being current the moment the document changes, a request is
    aborted the instant a newer one supersedes it on the same channel, and a
    stale response is reported as stale rather than as an ordinary failure —
    the distinction matters because a caller retries one and reports the other,
    and doing the wrong one makes a completion list either vanish or show an
    error nobody caused.
  - [x] A dependency outage is reported as an outage rather than as a build that
    produced nothing, and a target whose dependency failed is never started —
    proved by making the dependent build throw if it is ever reached.
  - [x] Storage failures are covered by the quota race leaving the last good
    save in place, an unreadable project being kept aside rather than replaced —
    the difference between "your work is unreadable" and "your work is gone",
    only one of which is true — and storage being unavailable being said rather
    than silently not saving. Backpressure is covered by the concurrency
    ceiling, which the limits contracts prove cannot be raised by asking for
    more.
  - [x] Evidence: 16 resilience contracts covering all of the above, alongside
    the existing cancellation, timeout and abort-signal contracts in the
    analysis worker client and the build runner.
- [ ] OPS-905 Run backup/restore and disaster-recovery exercise; record RPO/RTO
  evidence, integrity verification, and permission/audit restoration.
- [ ] OPS-906 Verify migration deploy/rollback or roll-forward procedure and
  compatibility across rolling versions.

### 11.2 Security and privacy assurance

- [ ] SEC-900 Resolve threat-model controls and rerun abuse cases for builds,
  parsers, emulators, storage, sharing, references, and administration.
- [ ] SEC-901 Run SAST, dependency/container/secret/licence scans, SBOM review,
  DAST, fuzz/property suites, and independent penetration test.
- [x] SEC-902 Verify CSP, worker/frame origins, message validation, CSRF, XSS,
  content disposition, upload types, archive containment, WebSocket origin/auth,
  authorization, rate/quota limits, and log redaction.
  - [x] The content security policy existed and had never been exercised, which
    is the common case and fails in the least useful way: everything works in
    development, where no policy is applied, and the first person to load the
    deployed build gets a blank page and a console full of violations. The
    release gate now serves the built application under the same headers the
    container serves, read from the nginx snippet rather than restated, and
    fails on any blocked resource. Blocked resources are reported only through
    the browser's security log, so without listening to it a policy that broke
    the application would have passed silently.
  - [x] Verified by breaking it. Forbidding the stylesheets the workbench loads
    made the gate fail with all three violations named, and restoring the
    policy made it pass again. A check that cannot fail is not a check.
  - [x] Both policies are audited against required directives, and every
    difference between them must be explained by name: the emulator runtime
    gets `unsafe-eval` because compiling machine code at run time is what an
    emulator is, and is framable by its own origin because the workbench frames
    it; the workbench gets neither. A difference with no recorded reason fails,
    because a relaxation nobody wrote down is one nobody reviewed. Inline
    script, a wildcard origin and an insecure origin are refused outright, and
    the document policy is checked to be the stricter of the two rather than
    the other way round.
  - [x] Every `message` listener in the source is checked to compare
    `event.origin` before acting. A handler that does not is one that will act
    on anything any page sends it, which is the whole shape of the attack, and
    a static check catches a new one the moment it is written.
  - [x] Archive containment, upload types and quota limits are covered by work
    already recorded: the zip reader refuses traversing names, absolute paths,
    symbolic links, encrypted entries and decompression bombs; imports are
    bounded by file count, per-file size and project total; and the backend
    publishes its own request, file, input, stage-time and artifact limits.
  - [x] Evidence: 8 header contracts covering both shipped policies, every
    refusal, the explained-difference register, and the document policy being
    stricter; 1 source-wide contract on message-origin checking; and the live
    policy check in the release gate, proved by deliberate breakage.
- [x] SEC-903 Verify no proprietary ROM/manual/commercial program, credential,
  registration code, user content, trace/memory dump, or secret is in repository,
  images, fixtures, logs, examples, or public bundles.
  - [x] The rule the gate enforced was one line long: no tracked file may have
    a firmware or disk-image extension. That is the most important case and it
    is not the only one — a release is also wrong if it carries a private key,
    an access token, or somebody's captured memory dump, and none of those
    announce themselves by their extension. The gate now runs a scanner over
    everything that would be published: firmware, disk and tape images;
    captured runtime state, which is a session and on a machine with real
    firmware loaded is also a copy of that firmware; private material such as a
    real `.env`, a key file or the local ROM directory; and eight credential
    shapes, each matched by a published marker — a key header, a vendor's token
    prefix — rather than guessed from a variable name, because a scanner that
    fires on the word `password` is one people learn to ignore.
  - [x] It reports where and what, never the value. A finding that quotes the
    token it found writes that token into the build log, which is a place
    secrets are read from, so the value is replaced by its shape.
  - [x] Nothing exempts itself. A fixture that must hold a key-shaped string is
    permitted only by an allowlist entry naming the file and the specific rule,
    and every entry must state why — an unexplained allowlist is how a real
    secret eventually gets one, so an entry without a reason is itself a
    finding. Path rules take no allowlist at all, because a firmware image has
    no legitimate reason to be in a source repository under any name.
  - [x] It scans what would be published rather than only what is tracked. This
    service is not yet committed, so a tracked-files scan would have checked
    nothing and reported success; the stage now lists tracked and unignored
    files together and fails outright if it finds nothing to scan. Third-party
    trees are excluded by name, because scanning them would bury a real finding
    in thousands of lines of someone else's fixtures. The built bundle is
    scanned as well as the source, since a value can reach the published output
    through an environment variable or an inlined import without ever appearing
    in a file.
  - [x] Running it found two matches, both examined and both corrections to the
    rules rather than suppressions: `.env.example` holds the name of one port
    variable and is documentation, so a template is now told apart from a real
    `.env` and is still scanned for content; and the bundle exporter's own test
    feeds an invented database connection string, credentials and all, to prove
    the exporter reports one, which the allowlist now names. Describing that
    fixture here originally reproduced the string, and the scanner caught the
    backlog document too, which is the check behaving exactly as intended.
  - [x] Evidence: 25 contracts covering every path rule and every credential
    shape, the value never appearing in a report, the reported line, the words
    and short values that must not fire, ordinary product source staying
    silent, the allowlist permitting one named rule in one named file and
    nothing else, every entry explaining itself, entries naming only rules that
    exist, and a scan that refuses to read a file it has already rejected. The
    gate and the contracts import the same scanner, so they cannot drift into
    disagreeing about what may be committed.
- [x] SEC-904 Publish vulnerability disclosure, dependency update, incident,
  retention, privacy, export, and deletion procedures.
  - [x] `docs/security-and-privacy.md` states what the product holds, what it
    does not, and what to do when something is wrong with it. Most of it is
    short because most of the usual answers do not apply: there is no account,
    no server holding anyone's work, and nothing collected about them.
  - [x] The absence of collection is stated by naming each thing rather than
    implied by a sentiment. "We respect your privacy" is not a claim anyone can
    check; "no analytics, no telemetry, no error reporting service, no font or
    script from another origin, no account" is, and each of those is verifiable
    against the content security policy the release gate serves the application
    under.
  - [x] Retention and deletion say where each kind of thing lives and how it
    leaves — your project in this browser's storage, firmware never anywhere but
    your machine, a folder written back only after permission is granted at the
    moment of saving, and source reaching the builder only inside a container
    with no network route. The one thing kept rather than removed is a project
    that could not be read, and it is kept because it is the person's work.
  - [x] Disclosure asks for a private report first, says what not to put in a
    public issue, and says what to expect in return — a policy that asks for
    reports and says nothing about the response is one people stop using. Scope
    is stated with reasons rather than as a way of avoiding the work: the
    emulated machine's own behaviour is the program author's, and a finding that
    needs the attacker to already control the browser is not the product's weak
    point.
  - [x] The incident procedure names the one case specific to this product,
    where the tempting response is the wrong one: if firmware or a credential
    ever reaches a published artefact, that is distribution, so the artefact is
    withdrawn rather than quietly replaced. It also requires a security fix to
    carry a regression test, because a security fix with no test is one that
    comes back.
  - [x] The outstanding GPL-2.0 position on the vendored ElkJS core is named
    here as well as in the decision index, because it is the kind of thing that
    is expensive to discover late.
  - [x] Evidence: 7 further documentation contracts covering the statement of
    where work lives, each named absence, the private reporting route and the
    stated response, every out-of-scope entry carrying a reason, the
    withdraw-rather-than-replace rule, the licence position being named, and
    every link resolving to a file that exists.

### 11.3 Accessibility and compatibility assurance

- [ ] A11Y-900 Complete automated and manual WCAG 2.2 AA audit of J-01–J-08 in
  dark/light/high-contrast, 200% zoom, 320 CSS px reflow, keyboard-only, reduced
  motion, and supported screen readers (QLT-005).
- [ ] A11Y-901 Resolve all A/AA blockers and document any AAA enhancements.
- [ ] A11Y-902 Verify structured alternatives for graphics, map, waveform,
  memory, trace, profiler, and hardware-register visualizations.
  - [x] The rule exists and runs in the release gate. A canvas, or a grid of
    coloured cells, carries information no assistive technology can read out of
    the pixels, and hiding it with `aria-hidden` is the right first step and
    not the whole answer: something has to carry the same information in text,
    near it, and be announced when it changes. A drawing surface therefore
    passes one of two ways — it names and describes itself, or it is hidden and
    its container holds a live region, caption or table saying what it shows.
    Anything else is a picture with nothing behind it.
  - [x] The pixel, screen and tile-map surfaces already met it: each hides its
    canvas and carries a live status naming the cursor position, the value
    under it, the layer, the palette in use and the keys that move and paint.
  - [x] Evidence: 6 rule contracts covering both passing shapes, both failing
    ones, a caption or table as the alternative, and the image role held to the
    same rule; and the gate refusing to pass if no drawing surface was on
    screen while the rule ran, because a check that finds nothing to check is
    not a passing check.
  - [ ] Coverage is the limitation and is stated rather than implied. The gate
    now opens a sample project before scanning, which raised the scan from 95
    controls to 319, but still reaches one drawing surface: the waveform,
    memory, trace, profiler and hardware-register views appear only once a
    build has run or a machine is executing, and the pixel canvases only once
    an asset document is chosen. Verifying those needs the gate to build and
    run before scanning. Until it does, this item stays open rather than
    claiming coverage it does not have.
- [ ] A11Y-903 Test current/previous major Chromium, Firefox, and Safari including
  file system, audio, WebAssembly, workers, full-screen, clipboard, gamepad,
  storage quota, and fallback paths (NFR-010).

### 11.4 Documentation and release evidence

- [x] DOC-900 Write architecture, ADR index, developer setup, dependency update,
  adapter authoring, schema migration, testing, and contribution documentation.
  - [x] `docs/architecture.md` describes how the product is put together and,
    more usefully, why, with the cost of each decision stated. A document that
    only lists what exists is a directory listing with prose around it. It
    covers the browser-versus-container split and why the one thing that cannot
    run in a tab is isolated rather than trusted; every module directory; the
    five rules the code follows, each recorded with the defect whose absence
    caused it; emulator and language adapter boundaries; schema migration;
    the three testing layers and why each exists; developer setup; how to add a
    machine, a toolchain, a language adapter or a limit; dependency and vendored
    -code policy; and how a contribution is considered complete.
  - [x] `docs/adr/README.md` indexes all nine decision records with their
    status, and says plainly which one is accepted technically but still
    awaiting a licence sign-off, because that is the thing a reader must not
    miss. It also says what makes a record worth writing: state what was
    rejected and what it would take to reverse, and always state the cost — a
    record that omits the cost is the one someone reverses without knowing it
    was there.
  - [x] Writing the index found two decision records both numbered 0006. A
    decision log whose identifiers are ambiguous cannot be cited, so the RISC OS
    toolchain record was renumbered to 0009, says so at its head, and the two
    references to it in the backlog were corrected. The Archimedes runtime keeps
    0006 because it was referenced first, and neither decision changed.
  - [x] Evidence: 10 documentation contracts covering unique record numbers,
    every record indexed and nothing indexed that is absent, a status recorded
    on each, the pending licence sign-off being visible in the index, every
    document link resolving, every module directory named and none invented,
    every `npm run` command named actually existing in the package, the README
    leading with the supported Compose command rather than the end-of-life one,
    and the firmware statement being present. A broken link, a missing record or
    a command that no longer exists are exactly the failures that mislead
    someone who trusted the document, and none of them announces itself.
- [ ] DOC-901 Write user guides for first run, target selection, ROM import,
  projects, builds, media, emulator/input, debugging/trace, tests, every asset
  editor, research, cloud/revisions/sharing, accessibility, and troubleshooting.
- [x] DOC-901A Ship those guides inside the IDE as searchable, deep-linkable,
  keyboard and screen-reader accessible help. Cover every visible operation with
  prerequisites, steps, expected result, limitations and recovery.
  - [x] Evidence: 3 help-topic contracts and 3 help-workspace contracts
   covering topic resolution, deep links and keyboard navigation, with the
   release gate's help stage verifying every topic file, screenshot reference
   and control name on every run.
- [x] DOC-901B Capture maintained screenshots from the shipped interface for all
  major workflows and operation groups. Provide captions, useful alternative
  text, responsive rendering, text equivalents and version metadata. Add checks
  for missing files, broken topic links and stale control names.
  - [x] Evidence: Covered by the release gate's help stage, which fails on a
   missing screenshot file, a broken topic link or a control name that no
   longer exists in the interface, so a stale screenshot cannot survive a
   rename.
- [x] DOC-901C Edit all in-app help into direct technical user-facing prose. Do
  not use em dashes or generic generated-assistant filler.
  Evidence: 19 maintained in-app topics, 15 screenshot references, topic and
  interaction tests, the pre-test help verifier, and desktop/narrow-screen
  browser acceptance against the container running on port 8090.
- [x] DOC-902 Publish machine/toolchain/emulator/media compatibility matrix,
  support tiers, known inaccuracies, upstream/licence acknowledgements, and
  project portability guarantees.
  - [x] `docs/compatibility.md` is generated from the catalogues the product
    actually runs on — the machine profiles, the adapter support map, the
    toolchain registry and the emulator adapter descriptors — rather than
    written by hand. A hand-maintained support table is a promise made once and
    then left behind by the code; a contract compares the checked-in document
    against what the generator produces, regenerates it when it differs, and
    fails, so a support claim cannot outlive the thing it describes.
  - [x] The tiers are defined in the document rather than assumed, because
    "supported" is the word most often stretched. Runnable means a qualified
    core in this build executes the machine with firmware the person supplies.
    Described means the product models the machine and no core here runs it,
    with nothing substituted for it. Of eleven registered machine profiles,
    five are runnable and six are described, and a described machine says what
    is missing — no ROM manifest registered, or no model in any core here —
    rather than naming a core beside a machine it will not run, which would
    read as a support claim.
  - [x] Capabilities are separated the same way. Fitted, preview and planned
    are three columns, not one, because planned means not fitted: it is listed
    because the machine has the hardware, not because this build does anything
    with it. A contract checks every capability of every machine appears in the
    column its declared state names, and that the three columns together
    account for all of them, so the document cannot quietly promote one.
  - [x] The known-inaccuracy list comes from the adapter support map's own
    per-machine limitation text, so it is the same sentence the product shows a
    user. Toolchains carry their version, language, processor, output kind and
    whether they run in the browser or the isolated container. The emulator
    adapters carry the operations each cannot do, including the twenty-four of
    thirty-six capabilities the vendored ElkJS core does not offer. Portability
    guarantees and the pointer to the licence and upstream register complete
    it, and the document states plainly that no firmware is distributed.
  - [x] Evidence: 6 contracts covering the checked-in document matching the
    generator, every machine being listed, no machine claiming to be runnable
    without an engine, agreement with the adapter support map about what runs,
    the capability separation, the firmware statement, and byte-for-byte
    determinism so the check is on content rather than ordering. `npm run
    compatibility` regenerates it.
- [x] DOC-903 Verify fresh-clone Docker Compose startup, migrations, seed-free
  operation, health, logs, test commands, and shutdown from documentation.
  - [x] Verified by running it rather than reading it, and it did not work. The
    documented `docker-compose up --build` failed at the image build: the
    production stage runs the project's build script, which typechecked the
    test files, and those import Node built-ins whose types a production
    install does not guarantee. The local gate never caught it because a
    development install has those types transitively — which is exactly the
    kind of accidental dependency that only shows up in a clean environment.
  - [x] Fixed at the right level rather than by installing more into the image.
    A production image must not typecheck tests, so the shipped application
    project now excludes them and a separate test project covers them with the
    Node types declared rather than inherited by accident. Building the product
    typechecks the product; checking the repository typechecks everything,
    tests included, and the release gate still runs the second. The image
    carries the referenced test project file without building it, because Vite
    resolves project references and a referenced file that is absent fails its
    HTML plugin.
  - [x] Splitting the projects brought the `scripts/` tests into the typechecked
    set for the first time, which found two script modules used by the gate with
    no type declarations at all. Both now have them.
  - [x] The second failure was in the documentation itself. The primary command
    named the standalone `docker-compose`, which is Compose v1, end of life, and
    fails against images built by current Docker with `KeyError:
    'ContainerConfig'` when it reuses a named volume — observed on this machine.
    The README now leads with the Compose plugin, says why, and tells anyone
    with only the old script to install the plugin rather than work around it.
  - [x] Evidence: a full lifecycle run against the built containers. Both
    services reached healthy from a clean start with no seed data and no
    migration step, `GET /healthz` returned 200 with `ok`, the workbench and the
    emulator frame both returned 200, and the two distinct content security
    policies were confirmed on the wire — the document policy on the workbench
    and the embedded policy carrying `unsafe-eval` on the runtime frame. Logs
    were readable through the documented command. The image contains no
    firmware, disk or tape image of any kind, and a firmware path falls through
    to the application shell rather than serving a file. A headless Chromium run
    against the running container measured five sizes down to 320 CSS pixels
    with no horizontal scrolling, nothing unreachable and no control clipped.
    `docker compose down` removed every container and network and left the port
    closed.
- [x] DOC-904 Generate traceability report mapping all in-scope requirements to
  automated/manual evidence, approved limitation, or accepted deferment.
  - [x] `docs/traceability.md` is generated from the backlog, which already
    carries the requirement identifiers and, under the ones that are done, what
    was built and how it was verified. A matrix maintained separately would be
    a second copy of that, and a second copy is a copy that disagrees.
  - [x] It reports three states rather than two, because two would be a lie in
    both directions. Traced means the requirement records its verification
    under an Evidence heading, where it can be found. Described means it names
    contracts, tests or a browser run in its prose but not under that heading —
    the work was done, the record is harder to follow. Untraced means neither,
    and that is the finding. A bare claim of having checked something is not
    accepted as either: a contract is a thing to go and look at, "thoroughly
    checked" is not.
  - [x] Across 294 tracked requirements: 118 complete, of which 27 are traced,
    56 described and 35 untraced, with 176 open. The 35 are listed by name
    rather than quietly counted, which is what makes the report worth having —
    a tick with nothing behind it looks like progress and is not, and the only
    way to see it is to count.
  - [x] The report is derived output and is regenerated on every run rather
    than compared against a checked-in copy, because the backlog changes with
    every completed requirement and a stale-file comparison would fail the gate
    on the very commit that did the work.
  - [x] Writing the parser exposed two real reading errors. A requirement whose
    title wraps across lines was being read as its first line only, which threw
    away the half that usually says how the work was verified, and made twenty
    items look untraced when they were not. An identifier with three segments —
    AST-INC-01 — was being read as two and its line skipped entirely, losing
    seven requirements from the count.
  - [x] Evidence: 12 contracts covering the requirement count against the
    backlog's own text, wrapped titles, an Evidence sub-item told apart from
    prose, part-counting on an open requirement, all four states including the
    refusal to accept a bare claim, the states adding up, every untraced
    requirement being listed and not merely counted, the statement that the
    report does not judge evidence quality, and determinism. `npm run
    traceability` regenerates it.
- [ ] DOC-905 Demonstrate J-01–J-08 and ACC-001–ACC-007 to stakeholders and
  capture acceptance.
- [x] DOC-906 Establish release/version/deprecation policy for APIs, schemas,
  profiles, adapters, toolchains, states, project bundles, and references.
  - [x] `docs/versioning-policy.md` is generated from the constants it
    describes. A policy that names version numbers goes stale the first time
    one of them changes, and a stale policy is worse than none: it is a
    commitment the product no longer keeps, in writing. Every number is
    imported from the module that defines it, and a contract fails the gate
    when the checked-in document stops matching.
  - [x] The policy is short because the product's rule is short. Every version
    of a document this product has ever written stays readable, and a document
    from a newer build is refused by name — saying which version it carries and
    which this build reads — rather than parsed as though the fields it does
    not contain were simply absent. A newer document is not a corrupt one, and
    the difference is the difference between "update the workbench" and "your
    project is broken".
  - [x] Nine versioned surfaces are covered with what each promises: the
    project document and its migration from version 1 forward, the portable
    bundle whose contents are verified before any migration runs, the build
    target schema, the toolchain registry that lets an artifact be traced to
    what produced it, the emulator adapter API where a mismatched adapter is
    not loaded at all — a partially-matching adapter being the one failure mode
    that produces wrong answers rather than no answers — analysis annotations
    bound to a digest rather than a filename, disk sets, settings, and the
    template catalogue.
  - [x] Deprecation is three steps and nothing is removed silently: marked with
    its replacement, kept working for at least one further release, then
    removed with anything still naming it reporting the removal by name. The
    policy states explicitly that a capability declared planned has never been
    fitted and is not a deprecation, because otherwise "planned" could be read
    as something once offered and withdrawn, which would be a support claim in
    reverse.
  - [x] Evidence: 6 contracts covering the checked-in document matching the
    generator, every stated version being read from its defining module rather
    than transcribed, no surface left without a promise, the newer-document
    wording, the planned-is-not-deprecated statement, and determinism.
    `npm run policy` regenerates it.

### Production release gate

- [ ] RELEASE-GATE All applicable acceptance criteria in Section 23 of the
  specification pass; no critical/high security issue, WCAG A/AA core-flow
  blocker, licence/ROM ambiguity, data-loss defect, or false machine-support
  claim remains; operational ownership and rollback are confirmed.

## 12. Post-release expansion order

Each item is a complete vertical slice and must pass the production machine
profile definition rather than reusing another target cosmetically.

- [ ] EXP-001 Complete remaining BBC Model A/B controller/regional profiles.
- [ ] EXP-002 Complete BBC B+ 64/128 profiles.
- [ ] EXP-003 Complete Electron expansions and storage profiles.
- [ ] EXP-004 Complete Master 128/Turbo/512/Compact profiles.
- [ ] EXP-005 Complete Atom storage/RAM/ROM profiles.
- [ ] EXP-006 Complete additional Tube processors one at a time with dual-CPU
  debugger and communication trace.
- [ ] EXP-007 Complete additional ARM2/ARM3 Archimedes profiles.
- [ ] EXP-008 Complete A4/A3010/A3020/A4000/A5000 tier.
- [ ] EXP-009 Complete Risc PC/A7000 tier without calling it Archimedes.
- [ ] EXP-010 Add additional toolchain dialects, media formats, expansion devices,
  trackers/codecs, and reference packs only through their adapter and legal gates.

## 13. Definition of done for every backlog item

An item is complete only when, as applicable:

- [ ] DOD-001 Behavior and boundaries match linked requirements and accepted design.
- [ ] DOD-002 Unit, contract, integration, E2E, accessibility, security, and
  performance tests proportional to risk are automated and passing.
- [ ] DOD-003 Keyboard, screen-reader semantics, zoom/reflow, theme/contrast,
  loading/error/empty/offline/stale/permission/unsupported states are addressed.
- [ ] DOD-004 Authorization is server-enforced and sensitive action auditing/
  redaction/retention is correct.
- [ ] DOD-005 Logs, metrics, traces, health, limits, cancellation, cleanup,
  upgrade, rollback/recovery, and runbook impact are addressed.
- [ ] DOD-006 Schemas/APIs include validation, compatibility, migrations, typed
  contracts, safe errors, and documentation.
- [ ] DOD-007 Third-party code/content/fixtures have version, licence,
  provenance, integrity, SBOM, and redistribution approval.
- [ ] DOD-008 Documentation and support/limitation matrices are updated.
- [ ] DOD-009 Requirement traceability and acceptance evidence are linked.
- [ ] DOD-010 No unrelated user work is overwritten and no secret/private or
  proprietary data is introduced.
